=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given three integers n, m, k. A good array arr of size n is defined as follows:

Each element in arr is in the inclusive range [1, m].
Exactly k indices i (where 1 <= i < n) satisfy the condition arr[i - 1] == arr[i].

Return the number of good arrays that can be formed.
Since the answer may be very large, return it modulo 10^9 + 7.
 
Example 1:

Input: n = 3, m = 2, k = 1
Output: 4
Explanation:

There are 4 good arrays. They are [1, 1, 2], [1, 2, 2], [2, 1, 1] and [2, 2, 1].
Hence, the answer is 4.


Example 2:

Input: n = 4, m = 2, k = 2
Output: 6
Explanation:

The good arrays are [1, 1, 1, 2], [1, 1, 2, 2], [1, 2, 2, 2], [2, 1, 1, 1], [2, 2, 1, 1] and [2, 2, 2, 1].
Hence, the answer is 6.


Example 3:

Input: n = 5, m = 2, k = 0
Output: 2
Explanation:

The good arrays are [1, 2, 1, 2, 1] and [2, 1, 2, 1, 2]. Hence, the answer is 2.


 
Constraints:

1 <= n <= 10^5
1 <= m <= 10^5
0 <= k <= n - 1

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def countGoodArrays(self, n: int, m: int, k: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

MOD = 10**9 + 7

class Solution:
    def countGoodArrays(self, n: int, m: int, k: int) -> int:
        # precompute factorials up to n-1
        max_fact = n  # maybe n
        fact = [1]*(max_fact+1)
        for i in range(1, max_fact+1):
            fact[i] = fact[i-1]*i % MOD
        invfact = [1]*(max_fact+1)
        invfact[max_fact] = pow(fact[max_fact], MOD-2, MOD)
        for i in range(max_fact, 0, -1):
            invfact[i-1] = invfact[i]*i % MOD
        
        def C(N, R):
            if R < 0 or R > N: return 0
            return fact[N]*invfact[R]%MOD*invfact[N-R]%MOD
        
        changes = n - 1 - k
        ways_positions = C(n-1, k)
        ways_values = m % MOD * pow((m-1)%MOD, changes, MOD) % MOD
        return ways_positions * ways_values % MOD

MOD = 10**9 + 7

class Solution:
    def countGoodArrays(self, n: int, m: int, k: int) -> int:
        N = n - 1
        fact = [1] * (N + 1)
        for i in range(1, N + 1):
            fact[i] = fact[i-1] * i % MOD
        invfact = [1] * (N + 1)
        invfact[N] = pow(fact[N], MOD - 2, MOD)
        for i in range(N, 0, -1):
            invfact[i-1] = invfact[i] * i % MOD
        
        def comb(nn, rr):
            if rr < 0 or rr > nn:
                return 0
            return fact[nn] * invfact[rr] % MOD * invfact[nn-rr] % MOD
        
        changes = N - k
        return comb(N, k) * (m % MOD) % MOD * pow((m - 1) % MOD, changes, MOD) % MOD

class Solution:
    def countGoodArrays(self, n: int, m: int, k: int) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        N = n - 1
        
        fact = [1] * (N + 1)
        for i in range(1, N + 1):
            fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD
            
        inv_fact = [1] * (N + 1)
        inv_fact[N] = pow(fact[N], MOD - 2, MOD)
        for i in range(N, 0, -1):
            inv_fact[i - 1] = inv_fact[i] * i % MOD
            
        def comb(a, b):
            if b < 0 or b > a:
                return 0
            return fact[a] * inv_fact[b] % MOD * inv_fact[a - b] % MOD
        
        changes = N - k
        return comb(N, k) * (m % MOD) % MOD * pow((m - 1) % MOD, changes, MOD) % MOD

MOD = 10**9 + 7
MAXN = 100000
_fact = [1]*(MAXN+1)
for i in range(1, MAXN+1): ...
_inv_fact = [1]*(MAXN+1)
_inv_fact[MAXN] = pow(_fact[MAXN], MOD-2, MOD)
for i in range(MAXN,0,-1): ...

class Solution:
    def countGoodArrays(...):
        N = n-1
        if k <0 or k>N: return0
        comb = _fact[N] * _inv_fact[k] % MOD * _inv_fact[N-k] % MOD
        changes = N-k
        return comb * (m % MOD) % MOD * pow((m-1)%MOD, changes, MOD) % MOD

The problem constraints n <= 1e5, one call. Per-call O(n) is fine. But if multiple calls, class-level cache is more efficient. We can implement lazy precompute inside method with class attributes to avoid top-level loops and still efficient.

Example:

class Solution:
    _fact = [1]
    _inv_fact = [1]
    _max_n = 0

    @classmethod
    def _ensure(cls, N):
        if N <= cls._max_n:
            return
        # extend fact up to N
        old = cls._max_n
        cls._fact.extend([1]*(N - old))
        for i in range(old+1, N+1):
            cls._fact[i] = cls._fact[i-1] * i % MOD
        cls._inv_fact.extend([1]*(N - old))
        cls._inv_fact[N] = pow(cls._fact[N], MOD-2, MOD)
        for i in range(N, old, -1):
            cls._inv_fact[i-1] = cls._inv_fact[i] * i % MOD
        cls._max_n = N

But this dynamic extension is more complex. Simpler global precompute to 100000. But if n=1e5, global precompute O(1e5) once. If multiple calls, good. If no calls, still O(1e5) at import, fine.

But to be robust, we can implement a lazy class-level cache with dynamic extension. Let's design it cleanly:

MOD = 10**9 + 7

class Solution:
    _fact = [1]
    _inv_fact = [1]
    _max_n = 0

    @classmethod
    def _ensure(cls, n: int) -> None:
        if n <= cls._max_n:
            return
        old = cls._max_n
        cls._fact.extend([1] * (n - old))
        for i in range(old + 1, n + 1):
            cls._fact[i] = cls._fact[i - 1] * i % MOD
        cls._inv_fact.extend([1] * (n - old))
        cls._inv_fact[n] = pow(cls._fact[n], MOD - 2, MOD)
        for i in range(n, old, -1):
            cls._inv_fact[i - 1] = cls._inv_fact[i] * i % MOD
        cls._max_n = n

    def countGoodArrays(self, n: int, m: int, k: int) -> int:
        N = n - 1
        if k < 0 or k > N:
            return 0
        self._ensure(N)
        comb = self._fact[N] * self._inv_fact[k] % MOD * self._inv_fact[N - k] % MOD
        changes = N - k
        return comb * (m % MOD) % MOD * pow((m - 1) % MOD, changes, MOD) % MOD

Need check dynamic extension correctness. Suppose _max_n=0, _fact=[1], _inv_fact=[1]. Ensure(N=5):
old=0, extend fact by 5 => length6, indices 0..5. Loop i=1..5 computes fact[1..5]. inv_fact extend by5 => length6, inv_fact[5]=pow(fact[5], MOD-2). Loop i=5 down to1: inv_fact[4]=inv_fact[5]*5, inv_fact[3]=inv_fact[4]*4, ..., inv_fact[0]=inv_fact[1]*1. This computes inverse factorial for all 0..5. Good. _max_n=5.
Suppose later Ensure(N=3): n<=_max_n return. Good.
Suppose later Ensure(N=8): old=5, extend fact by3 => length9, indices0..8. Loop i=6..8 computes fact[6..8]. inv_fact extend by3 => length9, inv_fact[8]=pow(fact[8], MOD-2). Loop i=8 down to6: inv_fact[7]=inv_fact[8]*8, inv_fact[6]=inv_fact[7]*7, inv_fact[5]=inv_fact[6]*6? Wait loop range(8, 5, -1) includes i=8,7,6. At i=6, inv_fact[5] = inv_fact[6] * 6. But inv_fact[5] already existed from previous computation and should equal inverse factorial 5. Does this update preserve it? Let's check: inv_fact[6] newly computed as inverse factorial 6. inv_fact[6]*6 mod MOD = 1/6! * 6 = 1/5! = inv_fact[5]. Yes, correct. So loop from n down to old+1 inclusive updates inv_fact[old] too, which is correct. But note if old=5, range(8,5,-1) gives 8,7,6, so it updates inv_fact[5], not inv_fact[4] etc. Good. It doesn't update inv_fact[0..4], but they remain correct. Good.
If old=0, loop range(5,0,-1) updates inv_fact[0] as well. Good.

This lazy cache is robust and no hardcoded max. But it's more code. The final answer can include class variables and classmethod. Is that acceptable? Yes. But maybe overkill. Maybe avoid classmethod complexity by using global precompute to 100000. That is simpler and no subclass issue. But if multiple calls, good. Let's think if top-level global precompute to 100000 is acceptable. It is simple:

MOD = 10**9 + 7
MAXN = 100000

fact = [1] * (MAXN + 1)
for i in range(1, MAXN + 1):
    fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD

inv_fact = [1] * (MAXN + 1)
inv_fact[MAXN] = pow(fact[MAXN], MOD - 2, MOD)
for i in range(MAXN, 0, -1):
    inv_fact[i - 1] = inv_fact[i] * i % MOD

class Solution:
    def countGoodArrays(self, n: int, m: int, k: int) -> int:
        N = n - 1
        if k < 0 or k > N:
            return 0
        comb = fact[N] * inv_fact[k] % MOD * inv_fact[N - k] % MOD
        changes = N - k
        return comb * (m % MOD) % MOD * pow((m - 1) % MOD, changes, MOD) % MOD

This is very clear. But if n=100000, N=99999, fact[100000] unused. If n=100000, fact[N] exists. If n=100000 and k=99999, inv_fact[99999] exists. Good. If n=100000 and k=0, inv_fact[99999] exists. Good.

Potential issue: If constraints n <= 10^5, but N=n-1 <= 99999. So MAXN=100000 is enough. If n=100000, fact[100000] exists but not needed. If n=100001 (not allowed), N=100000, fact[100000] exists, so actually supports n=100001. If n=100002, IndexError. Good.

But if the problem has n=10^5 and m=10^5, no issue. If the judge uses PyPy, global loops at import time okay.

Full code:

```python
class Solution:
    def countGoodArrays(self, n: int, m: int, k: int) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        N = n - 1
        
        if k < 0 or k > N:
            return 0
        
        fact = [1] * (N + 1)
        for i in range(1, N + 1):
            fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD
        
        inv_fact = [1] * (N + 1)
        inv_fact[N] = pow(fact[N], MOD - 2, MOD)
        for i in range(N, 0, -1):
            inv_fact[i - 1] = inv_fact[i] * i % MOD
        
        choose_positions = fact[N] * inv_fact[k] % MOD * inv_fact[N - k] % MOD
        changes = N - k
        
        return choose_positions * (m % MOD) % MOD * pow((m - 1) % MOD, changes, MOD) % MOD
```

Example1 n=3,m=2,k=1:
N=2, fact=[1,1,2], inv_fact[2]=inv(2)=500000004, loop i=2 inv_fact[1]=inv_fact[2]*2=1, i=1 inv_fact[0]=1. choose=fact[2]*inv_fact[1]*inv_fact[1]=2. changes=1. return2*2*2? Wait m%MOD=2, pow(1,1)=1, choose=2, product=4. Good.
Example2 n=4,m=2,k=2:
N=3, fact[3]=6, choose=fact3*inv2*inv1=6*inv2? inv_fact[2]=1/2, inv_fact[1]=1 =>3. changes=1, m=2 =>6. Good.
Example3 n=5,m=2,k=0:
N=4, choose=fact4*inv0*inv4=1, changes=4, m=2 =>2. Good.

</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
class Solution:
    def countGoodArrays(self, n: int, m: int, k: int) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        N = n - 1

        if k < 0 or k > N:
            return 0

        fact = [1] * (N + 1)
        for i in range(1, N + 1):
            fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD

        inv_fact = [1] * (N + 1)
        inv_fact[N] = pow(fact[N], MOD - 2, MOD)
        for i in range(N, 0, -1):
            inv_fact[i - 1] = inv_fact[i] * i % MOD

        choose_positions = fact[N] * inv_fact[k] % MOD * inv_fact[N - k] % MOD
        changes = N - k

        ans = choose_positions
        ans = ans * (m % MOD) % MOD
        ans = ans * pow((m - 1) % MOD, changes, MOD) % MOD
        return ans
```
